I’m not sure how to separate it.\n\nDoctor: If the pressures of owning the townhouse suddenly went away, do you think this feeling would mostly go away too?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, maybe it would quiet down a little if the townhouse stuff magically disappeared, because that’s where a lot of it sticks. Like every repair or bill feels like evidence that I’m not handling being an adult right.\n\nBut it’s not just the house, exactly. At work, too, if something goes wrong, I immediately feel like it’s my fault, like I’m a terrible employee. And then it all kind of blends together, like I’m a bad homeowner and I don’t deserve to keep what I worked for. I know that’s a lot from, like, a leaky faucet or an email mistake, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. Looking back, it’s kind of been there almost the whole time since all this started. Not always at the exact same level, but this low, heavy feeling, and not really being interested in things the way I used to.\n\nThere have been little stretches where I could function better, like I’d get through work or clean up a bit and think, okay, maybe I’m getting back to normal. But then it doesn’t really last. It’s more like brief breaks than it actually going away.\n\nDoctor: During these past two years, was there ever a stretch of two months or longer when the low mood fully went away?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there was ever a full two months where it really went away. There were times I was functioning better, like I’d get through my workday or answer messages or make food instead of just staring at the fridge, and I’d think maybe I was fine again.\n\nBut looking back, the low mood was still kind of there underneath, and I still wasn’t really interested in things the way I used to be. It was more like short breaks, not like it actually lifted for that long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Um… concentrating? I’m not totally sure how to answer that. Like, I can still do things sometimes, especially if there’s a deadline, but I also kind of drift.\n\nAt work I’ll open something and then somehow I’m thinking about whether I paid the electric bill, or that one cabinet door in the kitchen that never closes right, and then I’m not even looking at the spreadsheet anymore. But I don’t know if that counts as trouble concentrating or just… being stressed and scattered.\n\nI guess I’ve noticed it some, but it’s hard for me to tell how much is concentration versus just getting pulled into other thoughts.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I have. A lot, actually. It’s like if anything goes wrong, even something small, my brain immediately goes, well, that’s because you messed it up.

At work I keep thinking I’m a bad employee, even when I know other people make mistakes too. And with the house stuff, it’s worse, because I worked so hard to get it, and then if something needs fixing or I miss something or I don’t handle it right away, I start thinking I’m just… bad at being an adult, or a homeowner, like I don’t deserve to keep what I worked for.

And then I’ll get stuck on things that probably aren’t even related, like how the mailbox looks crooked or whether the neighbors think I’m letting everything fall apart, and it turns into this whole thing. But yeah, mostly I just feel like everything is my fault somehow.

I don’t know… “hopeless” feels like a big word, I guess. Sometimes I feel like things are never going to get easier, especially when I’m just trying to keep up with normal stuff and it all feels heavier than it should.

I’m not sure. Sometimes I can stop them if something else interrupts me, like a meeting starting or my phone ringing. But if I’m just sitting there, it kind of keeps looping.

I… don’t really know if it’s most days. I’m not good at keeping track like that. Some weeks it feels like it’s there all the time, and then I’ll have a day where I’m busy and I don’t notice it as much.

I’m not sure how to separate it.\n\nDoctor: If the pressures of owning the townhouse suddenly went away, do you think this feeling would mostly go away too?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, maybe it would quiet down a little if the townhouse stuff magically disappeared, because that’s where a lot of it sticks. Like every repair or bill feels like evidence that I’m not handling being an adult right.\n\nBut it’s not just the house, exactly. At work, too, if something goes wrong, I immediately feel like it’s my fault, like I’m a terrible employee. And then it all kind of blends together, like I’m a bad homeowner and I don’t deserve to keep what I worked for. I know that’s a lot from, like, a leaky faucet or an email mistake, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. Looking back, it’s kind of been there almost the whole time since all this started. Not always at the exact same level, but this low, heavy feeling, and not really being interested in things the way I used to.\n\nThere have been little stretches where I could function better, like I’d get through work or clean up a bit and think, okay, maybe I’m getting back to normal. But then it doesn’t really last. It’s more like brief breaks than it actually going away.\n\nDoctor: During these past two years, was there ever a stretch of two months or longer when the low mood fully went away?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there was ever a full two months where it really went away. There were times I was functioning better, like I’d get through my workday or answer messages or make food instead of just staring at the fridge, and I’d think maybe I was fine again.\n\nBut looking back, the low mood was still kind of there underneath, and I still wasn’t really interested in things the way I used to be. It was more like short breaks, not like it actually lifted for that long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Um… concentrating? I’m not totally sure how to answer that. Like, I can still do things sometimes, especially if there’s a deadline, but I also kind of drift.\n\nAt work I’ll open something and then somehow I’m thinking about whether I paid the electric bill, or that one cabinet door in the kitchen that never closes right, and then I’m not even looking at the spreadsheet anymore. But I don’t know if that counts as trouble concentrating or just… being stressed and scattered.\n\nI guess I’ve noticed it some, but it’s hard for me to tell how much is concentration versus just getting pulled into other thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling badly about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. A lot, actually. It’s like if anything goes wrong, even something small, my brain immediately goes, well, that’s because you messed it up.\n\nAt work I keep thinking I’m a bad employee, even when I know other people make mistakes too. And with the house stuff, it’s worse, because I worked so hard to get it, and then if something needs fixing or I miss something or I don’t handle it right away, I start thinking I’m just… bad at being an adult, or a homeowner, like I don’t deserve to keep what I worked for.\n\nAnd then I’ll get stuck on things that probably aren’t even related, like how the mailbox looks crooked or whether the neighbors think I’m letting everything fall apart, and it turns into this whole thing. But yeah, mostly I just feel like everything is my fault somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “hopeless” feels like a big word, I guess. Sometimes I feel like things are never going to get easier, especially when I’m just trying to keep up with normal stuff and it all feels heavier than it should.\n\nBut then I also get distracted by little things, like I’ll start thinking if I just organize one drawer or return one email then maybe I’ll feel less stuck, and then I’m thinking about office supplies for some reason. So… I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly. Maybe kind of discouraged? Like I can’t always see how it’s supposed to get better.\n\nDoctor: When these worries start, is it hard for you to stop or control them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Sometimes I can stop them if something else interrupts me, like a meeting starting or my phone ringing. But if I’m just sitting there, it kind of keeps looping.\n\nAlthough… I don’t always know if I’m trying to control it or if I’m just following the thought, you know? Like I’ll think about one thing, then I’m thinking about something completely different, like whether I need more trash bags or if I forgot to move laundry, and then I’m not even sure what the original worry was.\n\nSo… maybe it is hard sometimes, but I don’t know how much. It’s not like I can measure it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worried like this on most days?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know if it’s most days. I’m not good at keeping track like that. Some weeks it feels like it’s there all the time, and then I’ll have a day where I’m busy and I don’t notice it as much.\n\nI guess mornings are weird because I’ll start thinking about what I have to do, but then I’m also thinking about whether I left dishes in the sink or if my shoes by the door look messy, and then I’m late because I’m looking for my badge. So it’s hard for me to say if that counts as “worried” or just… my brain being loud.\n\nMaybe it happens a lot, but I don’t want to say “most days” if I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I don’t know if I’d say all the time, but there are these moments where I realize I’ve been walking the same little route in my apartment, like from the kitchen to the front door and back again.\n\nAnd I’ll be rubbing my thumb against my fingers, like this repetitive thing, and checking the peephole even though nothing happened. It’s not like there’s a reason, exactly. I’ll just… keep doing it.   },

I’m not really sure how to measure that. I mean, I get tired, but I also don’t know if that’s low energy or just… the day being too much.

I know that’s a lot from, like, a leaky faucet or an email mistake, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. Looking back, it’s kind of been there almost the whole time since all this started. Not always at the exact same level, but this low, heavy feeling, and not really being interested in things the way I used to.\n\nThere have been little stretches where I could function better, like I’d get through work or clean up a bit and think, okay, maybe I’m getting back to normal. But then it doesn’t really last. It’s more like brief breaks than it actually going away.\n\nDoctor: During these past two years, was there ever a stretch of two months or longer when the low mood fully went away?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there was ever a full two months where it really went away. There were times I was functioning better, like I’d get through my workday or answer messages or make food instead of just staring at the fridge, and I’d think maybe I was fine again.\n\nBut looking back, the low mood was still kind of there underneath, and I still wasn’t really interested in things the way I used to be. It was more like short breaks, not like it actually lifted for that long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Um… concentrating? I’m not totally sure how to answer that. Like, I can still do things sometimes, especially if there’s a deadline, but I also kind of drift.\n\nAt work I’ll open something and then somehow I’m thinking about whether I paid the electric bill, or that one cabinet door in the kitchen that never closes right, and then I’m not even looking at the spreadsheet anymore. But I don’t know if that counts as trouble concentrating or just… being stressed and scattered.\n\nI guess I’ve noticed it some, but it’s hard for me to tell how much is concentration versus just getting pulled into other thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling badly about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. A lot, actually. It’s like if anything goes wrong, even something small, my brain immediately goes, well, that’s because you messed it up.\n\nAt work I keep thinking I’m a bad employee, even when I know other people make mistakes too. And with the house stuff, it’s worse, because I worked so hard to get it, and then if something needs fixing or I miss something or I don’t handle it right away, I start thinking I’m just… bad at being an adult, or a homeowner, like I don’t deserve to keep what I worked for.\n\nAnd then I’ll get stuck on things that probably aren’t even related, like how the mailbox looks crooked or whether the neighbors think I’m letting everything fall apart, and it turns into this whole thing. But yeah, mostly I just feel like everything is my fault somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “hopeless” feels like a big word, I guess. Sometimes I feel like things are never going to get easier, especially when I’m just trying to keep up with normal stuff and it all feels heavier than it should.\n\nBut then I also get distracted by little things, like I’ll start thinking if I just organize one drawer or return one email then maybe I’ll feel less stuck, and then I’m thinking about office supplies for some reason. So… I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly. Maybe kind of discouraged? Like I can’t always see how it’s supposed to get better.\n\nDoctor: When these worries start, is it hard for you to stop or control them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Sometimes I can stop them if something else interrupts me, like a meeting starting or my phone ringing. But if I’m just sitting there, it kind of keeps looping.\n\nAlthough… I don’t always know if I’m trying to control it or if I’m just following the thought, you know? Like I’ll think about one thing, then I’m thinking about something completely different, like whether I need more trash bags or if I forgot to move laundry, and then I’m not even sure what the original worry was.\n\nSo… maybe it is hard sometimes, but I don’t know how much. It’s not like I can measure it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worried like this on most days?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know if it’s most days. I’m not good at keeping track like that. Some weeks it feels like it’s there all the time, and then I’ll have a day where I’m busy and I don’t notice it as much.\n\nI guess mornings are weird because I’ll start thinking about what I have to do, but then I’m also thinking about whether I left dishes in the sink or if my shoes by the door look messy, and then I’m late because I’m looking for my badge. So it’s hard for me to say if that counts as “worried” or just… my brain being loud.\n\nMaybe it happens a lot, but I don’t want to say “most days” if I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I don’t know if I’d say all the time, but there are these moments where I realize I’ve been walking the same little route in my apartment, like from the kitchen to the front door and back again.\n\nAnd I’ll be rubbing my thumb against my fingers, like this repetitive thing, and checking the peephole even though nothing happened. It’s not like there’s a reason, exactly. I’ll just… keep doing it. Then I notice I’ve been doing it for a while and I feel kind of embarrassed, because I meant to be doing something else, like putting a cup away or getting my bag ready.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite being lower or higher than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been kind of all over the place. Mostly lower during the day, I think. Like I’ll mean to eat lunch and then I just have coffee, or maybe toast, and then suddenly it’s later than I thought.\n\nSometimes I don’t notice until I’m trying to get dressed and my pants feel a little looser, and then I’m like, oh, I guess I haven’t really been eating actual meals. I keep buying things that are easy, but then I don’t make them, or I forget they’re there, and then I just eat whatever is closest. Coffee, toast, crackers, stuff like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling low on energy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to measure that. I mean, I get tired, but I also don’t know if that’s low energy or just… the day being too much.\n\nLike at work, sometimes I’ll sit down and open the thing I’m supposed to do, and then I end up rearranging the little sticky notes near my keyboard because one of them is curling up, and then I realize I haven’t actually started. But that’s not exactly energy, I guess.\n\nI haven’t really noticed it in a clear way, like “oh, I have no energy today.” It’s more… I lose momentum, maybe. Does that count?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Does that count?\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been having trouble sleeping?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 27 / business_operations_specialist / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Sleep_Disturbance\nmanifestation: \"Most nights I lie awake for hours listening to the house settle, then wake up around three or four and can’t get back to sleep before work.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… sleep has been pretty bad. Most nights I’ll get in bed and then I’m just lying there for hours, listening to the house make those little settling noises — like the pipes or the walls, I don’t know. I’ll think, okay, if I fall asleep now I can still get enough, and then I look again and it’s way later.

And then even if I do sleep, I wake up around three or four a lot. That’s the worst part, because it’s not like I can just roll over and go back to sleep before work. I just lie there waiting for morning, basically.

Like every repair or bill feels like evidence that I’m not handling being an adult right.\n\nBut it’s not just the house, exactly. And then it all kind of blends together, like I’m a bad homeowner and I don’t deserve to keep what I worked for. I know that’s a lot from, like, a leaky faucet or an email mistake, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. Looking back, it’s kind of been there almost the whole time since all this started. Not always at the exact same level, but this low, heavy feeling, and not really being interested in things the way I used to.\n\nThere have been little stretches where I could function better, like I’d get through work or clean up a bit and think, okay, maybe I’m getting back to normal. But then it doesn’t really last. It’s more like brief breaks than it actually going away.\n\nDoctor: During these past two years, was there ever a stretch of two months or longer when the low mood fully went away?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there was ever a full two months where it really went away. There were times I was functioning better, like I’d get through my workday or answer messages or make food instead of just staring at the fridge, and I’d think maybe I was fine again.\n\nBut looking back, the low mood was still kind of there underneath, and I still wasn’t really interested in things the way I used to be. It was more like short breaks, not like it actually lifted for that long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Um… concentrating? I’m not totally sure how to answer that. Like, I can still do things sometimes, especially if there’s a deadline, but I also kind of drift.\n\nAt work I’ll open something and then somehow I’m thinking about whether I paid the electric bill, or that one cabinet door in the kitchen that never closes right, and then I’m not even looking at the spreadsheet anymore. But I don’t know if that counts as trouble concentrating or just… being stressed and scattered.\n\nI guess I’ve noticed it some, but it’s hard for me to tell how much is concentration versus just getting pulled into other thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling badly about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. A lot, actually. It’s like if anything goes wrong, even something small, my brain immediately goes, well, that’s because you messed it up.\n\nAt work I keep thinking I’m a bad employee, even when I know other people make mistakes too. And with the house stuff, it’s worse, because I worked so hard to get it, and then if something needs fixing or I miss something or I don’t handle it right away, I start thinking I’m just… bad at being an adult, or a homeowner, like I don’t deserve to keep what I worked for.\n\nAnd then I’ll get stuck on things that probably aren’t even related, like how the mailbox looks crooked or whether the neighbors think I’m letting everything fall apart, and it turns into this whole thing. But yeah, mostly I just feel like everything is my fault somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “hopeless” feels like a big word, I guess. Sometimes I feel like things are never going to get easier, especially when I’m just trying to keep up with normal stuff and it all feels heavier than it should.\n\nBut then I also get distracted by little things, like I’ll start thinking if I just organize one drawer or return one email then maybe I’ll feel less stuck, and then I’m thinking about office supplies for some reason. So… I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly. Maybe kind of discouraged? Like I can’t always see how it’s supposed to get better.\n\nDoctor: When these worries start, is it hard for you to stop or control them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. It’s not like I can measure it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worried like this on most days?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know if it’s most days. I’m not good at keeping track like that. better.\n- key: additional_requirement_3\n  name: Additional Requirement 3\n  description: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n  manifestation: There was a stretch of several weeks when my mood was not as heavy and I was getting through work more normally, but I still heard the voice near the vents and stayed convinced the mortgage company and my coworkers were monitoring me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve missed social plans, let the townhouse get messier than I can stand, and made enough mistakes at work that my supervisor started checking in on my performance. I’m still working, but it takes much more effort to keep up with basic responsibilities.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about two years and three months, starting after I bought the townhouse and the pressure of keeping it began to build.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 1 year and 8 months\n  manifestation: The voices and fixed fears about being watched have been present for about a year and eight months.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 2 year and 1 month\n  manifestation: The depression and loss of interest have been with me for about two years and one month.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 27 / business_operations_specialist / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S030\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite being lower or higher than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been kind of all over the place. Mostly lower during the day, I think. Like I’ll mean to eat lunch and then I just have coffee, or maybe toast, and then suddenly it’s later than I thought.\n\nSometimes I don’t notice until I’m trying to get dressed and my pants feel a little looser, and then I’m like, oh, I guess I haven’t really been eating actual meals. I keep buying things that are easy, but then I don’t make them, or I forget they’re there, and then I just eat whatever is closest. Coffee, toast, crackers, stuff like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling low on energy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to measure that. And then it all kind of blends together, like I’m a bad homeowner and I don’t deserve to keep what I worked for. I know that’s a lot from, like, a leaky faucet or an email mistake, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. Looking back, it’s kind of been there almost the whole time since all this started. Not always at the exact same level, but this low, heavy feeling, and not really being interested in things the way I used to.\n\nThere have been little stretches where I could function better, like I’d get through work or clean up a bit and think, okay, maybe I’m getting back to normal. But then it doesn’t really last. It’s more like brief breaks than it actually going away.\n\nDoctor: During these past two years, was there ever a stretch of two months or longer when the low mood fully went away?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there was ever a full two months where it really went away. There were times I was functioning better, like I’d get through my workday or answer messages or make food instead of just staring at the fridge, and I’d think maybe I was fine again.\n\nBut looking back, the low mood was still kind of there underneath, and I still wasn’t really interested in things the way I used to be. It was more like short breaks, not like it actually lifted for that long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Um… concentrating? But I don’t know if that counts as trouble concentrating or just… being stressed and scattered.\n\nI guess I’ve noticed it some, but it’s hard for me to tell how much is concentration versus just getting pulled into other thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling badly about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. A lot, actually. But yeah, mostly I just feel like everything is my fault somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “hopeless” feels like a big word, I guess. Sometimes I feel like things are never going to get easier, especially when I’m just trying to keep up with normal stuff and it all feels heavier than it should.\n\nBut then I also get distracted by little things, like I’ll start thinking if I just organize one drawer or return one email then maybe I’ll feel less stuck, and then I’m thinking about office supplies for some reason. So… I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly. Maybe kind of discouraged? Like I can’t always see how it’s supposed to get better.\n\nDoctor: When these worries start, is it hard for you to stop or control them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Sometimes I can stop them if something else interrupts me, like a meeting starting or my phone ringing. Like I’ll think about one thing, then I’m thinking about something completely different, like whether I need more trash bags or if I forgot to move laundry, and then I’m not even sure what the original worry was.\n\nSo… maybe it is hard sometimes, but I don’t know how much. It’s not like I can measure it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worried like this on most days?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know if it’s most days. I’m not good at keeping track like that. Some weeks it feels like it’s there all the time, and then I’ll have a day where I’m busy and I don’t notice it as much.\n\nI guess mornings are weird because I’ll start thinking about what I have to do, but then I’m also thinking about whether I left dishes in the sink or if my shoes by the door look messy, and then I’m late because I’m looking for my badge. So it’s hard for me to say if that counts as “worried” or just… my brain being loud.\n\nMaybe it happens a lot, but I don’t want to say “most days” if I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I don’t know if I’d say all the time, but there are these moments where I realize I’ve been walking the same little route in my apartment, like from the kitchen to the front door and back again.\n\nAnd I’ll be rubbing my thumb against my fingers, like this repetitive thing, and checking the peephole even though nothing happened. It’s not like there’s a reason, exactly. I’ll just… keep doing it. Then I notice I’ve been doing it for a while and I feel kind of embarrassed, because I meant to be doing something else, like putting a cup away or getting my bag ready.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite being lower or higher than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been kind of all over the place. I keep buying things that are easy, but then I don’t make them, or I forget they’re there, and then I just eat whatever is closest. But that’s not exactly energy, I guess.\n\nI haven’t really noticed it in a clear way, like “oh, I have no energy today.” It’s more… I lose momentum, maybe. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sleep has been pretty bad. What brings you in?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I came in because I can’t get this feeling to stop, like anything that goes wrong is somehow my fault. At work I’ll think, “I’m a terrible employee,” even if it’s probably not all on me, and then at home it turns into this whole thing where I feel like I’m a bad homeowner too, like I don’t deserve to keep what I worked for.\n\nSorry, I know that sounds dramatic. I just keep going in circles about it. Like I’ll notice one small thing that needs fixing or one mistake at work, and then suddenly it feels like proof that I’m failing at everything.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a particular stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it kind of started after I bought my townhouse. Not like the exact day or anything, but once the pressure of keeping up with it started building, it just… stuck.\n\nIt’s been going on for about two years and maybe three months now. Which sounds so specific, I know, but I remember because I kept thinking buying the place was supposed to make me feel settled, and instead I started worrying about every little repair and bill, and then that somehow turned into this bigger feeling that I was messing everything up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different parts of your life, not just your home and work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I know the home and work stuff are the loudest in my head. Sometimes other things get pulled into it, like if I forget to call someone back or I see some random email and think I handled it wrong… but I don’t know if that counts as “many different parts.”\n\nSorry, I’m kind of thinking of this one time I spent like an hour deciding whether to reply “thanks” or “thank you” to someone, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud. But mostly I notice it with responsibility stuff, I guess. I’m not sure how to separate it.\n\nDoctor: If the pressures of owning the townhouse suddenly went away, do you think this feeling would mostly go away too?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, maybe it would quiet down a little if the townhouse stuff magically disappeared, because that’s where a lot of it sticks. Like every repair or bill feels like evidence that I’m not handling being an adult right.\n\nBut it’s not just the house, exactly. At work, too, if something goes wrong, I immediately feel like it’s my fault, like I’m a terrible employee. And then it all kind of blends together, like I’m a bad homeowner and I don’t deserve to keep what I worked for. I know that’s a lot from, like, a leaky faucet or an email mistake, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. Looking back, it’s kind of been there almost the whole time since all this started. Not always at the exact same level, but this low, heavy feeling, and not really being interested in things the way I used to.\n\nThere have been little stretches where I could function better, like I’d get through work or clean up a bit and think, okay, maybe I’m getting back to normal. But then it doesn’t really last. It’s more like brief breaks than it actually going away.\n\nDoctor: During these past two years, was there ever a stretch of two months or longer when the low mood fully went away?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there was ever a full two months where it really went away. There were times I was functioning better, like I’d get through my workday or answer messages or make food instead of just staring at the fridge, and I’d think maybe I was fine again.\n\nBut looking back, the low mood was still kind of there underneath, and I still wasn’t really interested in things the way I used to be. It was more like short breaks, not like it actually lifted for that long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Um… concentrating? I’m not totally sure how to answer that. Like, I can still do things sometimes, especially if there’s a deadline, but I also kind of drift.\n\nAt work I’ll open something and then somehow I’m thinking about whether I paid the electric bill, or that one cabinet door in the kitchen that never closes right, and then I’m not even looking at the spreadsheet anymore. But I don’t know if that counts as trouble concentrating or just… being stressed and scattered.\n\nI guess I’ve noticed it some, but it’s hard for me to tell how much is concentration versus just getting pulled into other thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling badly about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. A lot, actually. It’s like if anything goes wrong, even something small, my brain immediately goes, well, that’s because you messed it up.\n\nAt work I keep thinking I’m a bad employee, even when I know other people make mistakes too. And with the house stuff, it’s worse, because I worked so hard to get it, and then if something needs fixing or I miss something or I don’t handle it right away, I start thinking I’m just… bad at being an adult, or a homeowner, like I don’t deserve to keep what I worked for.\n\nAnd then I’ll get stuck on things that probably aren’t even related, like how the mailbox looks crooked or whether the neighbors think I’m letting everything fall apart, and it turns into this whole thing. But yeah, mostly I just feel like everything is my fault somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “hopeless” feels like a big word, I guess. Sometimes I feel like things are never going to get easier, especially when I’m just trying to keep up with normal stuff and it all feels heavier than it should.\n\nBut then I also get distracted by little things, like I’ll start thinking if I just organize one drawer or return one email then maybe I’ll feel less stuck, and then I’m thinking about office supplies for some reason. So… I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly. Maybe kind of discouraged? Like I can’t always see how it’s supposed to get better.\n\nDoctor: When these worries start, is it hard for you to stop or control them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Sometimes I can stop them if something else interrupts me, like a meeting starting or my phone ringing. But if I’m just sitting there, it kind of keeps looping.\n\nAlthough… I don’t always know if I’m trying to control it or if I’m just following the thought, you know? Like I’ll think about one thing, then I’m thinking about something completely different, like whether I need more trash bags or if I forgot to move laundry, and then I’m not even sure what the original worry was.\n\nSo… maybe it is hard sometimes, but I don’t know how much. It’s not like I can measure it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worried like this on most days?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know if it’s most days. I’m not good at keeping track like that. Some weeks it feels like it’s there all the time, and then I’ll have a day where I’m busy and I don’t notice it as much.\n\nI guess mornings are weird because I’ll start thinking about what I have to do, but then I’m also thinking about whether I left dishes in the sink or if my shoes by the door look messy, and then I’m late because I’m looking for my badge. So it’s hard for me to say if that counts as “worried” or just… my brain being loud.\n\nMaybe it happens a lot, but I don’t want to say “most days” if I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I don’t know if I’d say all the time, but there are these moments where I realize I’ve been walking the same little route in my apartment, like from the kitchen to the front door and back again.\n\nAnd I’ll be rubbing my thumb against my fingers, like this repetitive thing, and checking the peephole even though nothing happened. It’s not like there’s a reason, exactly. I’ll just… keep doing it. Then I notice I’ve been doing it for a while and I feel kind of embarrassed, because I meant to be doing something else, like putting a cup away or getting my bag ready.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite being lower or higher than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been kind of all over the place. Mostly lower during the day, I think. Like I’ll mean to eat lunch and then I just have coffee, or maybe toast, and then suddenly it’s later than I thought.\n\nSometimes I don’t notice until I’m trying to get dressed and my pants feel a little looser, and then I’m like, oh, I guess I haven’t really been eating actual meals. I keep buying things that are easy, but then I don’t make them, or I forget they’re there, and then I just eat whatever is closest. Coffee, toast, crackers, stuff like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling low on energy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to measure that. I mean, I get tired, but I also don’t know if that’s low energy or just… the day being too much.\n\nLike at work, sometimes I’ll sit down and open the thing I’m supposed to do, and then I end up rearranging the little sticky notes near my keyboard because one of them is curling up, and then I realize I haven’t actually started. But that’s not exactly energy, I guess.\n\nI haven’t really noticed it in a clear way, like “oh, I have no energy today.” It’s more… I lose momentum, maybe. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sleep has been pretty bad. Most nights I’ll get in bed and then I’m just lying there for hours, listening to the house make those little settling noises — like the pipes or the walls, I don’t know. I’ll think, okay, if I fall asleep now I can still get enough, and then I look again and it’s way later.\n\nAnd then even if I do sleep, I wake up around three or four a lot. That’s the worst part, because it’s not like I can just roll over and go back to sleep before work. I just lie there waiting for morning, basically.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, sometimes when I’m really tired I think, like, I don’t want to keep doing the day over and over, if that makes sense, but then I start thinking about work emails or whether I paid my electric bill, and it gets kind of mixed up.\n\nI haven’t really sat down and made it into a… specific thought, I guess. “Hurting myself” sounds very direct, and I don’t know. I don’t think I can say it that way. It’s more like I get overwhelmed and want everything to stop for a while, but then I’ll be looking at my phone or folding a towel or something.\n\nDoctor: During this time, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when your depression felt clearly worse than usual most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s hard because it doesn’t feel like it started and stopped neatly, like a calendar block. It’s more like it settled in and stayed.\n\nThe depression and not really being interested in things has been there for… about two years and one month, I guess. Which sounds weirdly specific, but I remember because it lines up with when I kept thinking, “maybe this is just a rough month,” and then it wasn’t just a month.\n\nSo, yes, definitely more than two weeks. It’s been kind of most days for a long time.\n\nDoctor: During this time, was there ever a period when you felt clearly more depressed than your usual baseline?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s kind of hard for me to separate “worse than baseline” because the baseline itself has been low for so long. Like, it’s not like I was fine and then there was one obvious dip.\n\nThe depression and not wanting to do things, or not feeling much interest, has been around for about two years and one month. I remember thinking at first it was just a bad stretch, like when work gets busy and you stop doing normal stuff, but then it just… kept being there. So yes, there have been times it felt heavier, but honestly the whole thing has felt pretty consistently depressed for a long time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Yeah… most of the time, honestly. I came in partly because I can’t stop feeling like everything that goes wrong is somehow my fault.

I just keep going in circles about it. Like I’ll notice one small thing that needs fixing or one mistake at work, and then suddenly it feels like proof that I’m failing at everything.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after a particular stressful event?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it kind of started after I bought my townhouse. Not like the exact day or anything, but once the pressure of keeping up with it started building, it just… stuck.\n\nIt’s been going on for about two years and maybe three months now. Which sounds so specific, I know, but I remember because I kept thinking buying the place was supposed to make me feel settled, and instead I started worrying about every little repair and bill, and then that somehow turned into this bigger feeling that I was messing everything up.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different parts of your life, not just your home and work?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I know the home and work stuff are the loudest in my head. Sometimes other things get pulled into it, like if I forget to call someone back or I see some random email and think I handled it wrong… but I don’t know if that counts as “many different parts.”\n\nSorry, I’m kind of thinking of this one time I spent like an hour deciding whether to reply “thanks” or “thank you” to someone, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud. But mostly I notice it with responsibility stuff, I guess. I’m not sure how to separate it.\n\nDoctor: If the pressures of owning the townhouse suddenly went away, do you think this feeling would mostly go away too?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, maybe it would quiet down a little if the townhouse stuff magically disappeared, because that’s where a lot of it sticks. Like every repair or bill feels like evidence that I’m not handling being an adult right.\n\nBut it’s not just the house, exactly. At work, too, if something goes wrong, I immediately feel like it’s my fault, like I’m a terrible employee. And then it all kind of blends together, like I’m a bad homeowner and I don’t deserve to keep what I worked for. I know that’s a lot from, like, a leaky faucet or an email mistake, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. Looking back, it’s kind of been there almost the whole time since all this started. Not always at the exact same level, but this low, heavy feeling, and not really being interested in things the way I used to.\n\nThere have been little stretches where I could function better, like I’d get through work or clean up a bit and think, okay, maybe I’m getting back to normal. But then it doesn’t really last. It’s more like brief breaks than it actually going away.\n\nDoctor: During these past two years, was there ever a stretch of two months or longer when the low mood fully went away?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think there was ever a full two months where it really went away. There were times I was functioning better, like I’d get through my workday or answer messages or make food instead of just staring at the fridge, and I’d think maybe I was fine again.\n\nBut looking back, the low mood was still kind of there underneath, and I still wasn’t really interested in things the way I used to be. It was more like short breaks, not like it actually lifted for that long.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Um… concentrating? I’m not totally sure how to answer that. Like, I can still do things sometimes, especially if there’s a deadline, but I also kind of drift.\n\nAt work I’ll open something and then somehow I’m thinking about whether I paid the electric bill, or that one cabinet door in the kitchen that never closes right, and then I’m not even looking at the spreadsheet anymore. But I don’t know if that counts as trouble concentrating or just… being stressed and scattered.\n\nI guess I’ve noticed it some, but it’s hard for me to tell how much is concentration versus just getting pulled into other thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling badly about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. A lot, actually. It’s like if anything goes wrong, even something small, my brain immediately goes, well, that’s because you messed it up.\n\nAt work I keep thinking I’m a bad employee, even when I know other people make mistakes too. And with the house stuff, it’s worse, because I worked so hard to get it, and then if something needs fixing or I miss something or I don’t handle it right away, I start thinking I’m just… bad at being an adult, or a homeowner, like I don’t deserve to keep what I worked for.\n\nAnd then I’ll get stuck on things that probably aren’t even related, like how the mailbox looks crooked or whether the neighbors think I’m letting everything fall apart, and it turns into this whole thing. But yeah, mostly I just feel like everything is my fault somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “hopeless” feels like a big word, I guess. Sometimes I feel like things are never going to get easier, especially when I’m just trying to keep up with normal stuff and it all feels heavier than it should.\n\nBut then I also get distracted by little things, like I’ll start thinking if I just organize one drawer or return one email then maybe I’ll feel less stuck, and then I’m thinking about office supplies for some reason. So… I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly. Maybe kind of discouraged? Like I can’t always see how it’s supposed to get better.\n\nDoctor: When these worries start, is it hard for you to stop or control them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Sometimes I can stop them if something else interrupts me, like a meeting starting or my phone ringing. But if I’m just sitting there, it kind of keeps looping.\n\nAlthough… I don’t always know if I’m trying to control it or if I’m just following the thought, you know? Like I’ll think about one thing, then I’m thinking about something completely different, like whether I need more trash bags or if I forgot to move laundry, and then I’m not even sure what the original worry was.\n\nSo… maybe it is hard sometimes, but I don’t know how much. It’s not like I can measure it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worried like this on most days?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know if it’s most days. I’m not good at keeping track like that. Some weeks it feels like it’s there all the time, and then I’ll have a day where I’m busy and I don’t notice it as much.\n\nI guess mornings are weird because I’ll start thinking about what I have to do, but then I’m also thinking about whether I left dishes in the sink or if my shoes by the door look messy, and then I’m late because I’m looking for my badge. So it’s hard for me to say if that counts as “worried” or just… my brain being loud.\n\nMaybe it happens a lot, but I don’t want to say “most days” if I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I don’t know if I’d say all the time, but there are these moments where I realize I’ve been walking the same little route in my apartment, like from the kitchen to the front door and back again.\n\nAnd I’ll be rubbing my thumb against my fingers, like this repetitive thing, and checking the peephole even though nothing happened. It’s not like there’s a reason, exactly. I’ll just… keep doing it. Then I notice I’ve been doing it for a while and I feel kind of embarrassed, because I meant to be doing something else, like putting a cup away or getting my bag ready.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite being lower or higher than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been kind of all over the place. Mostly lower during the day, I think. Like I’ll mean to eat lunch and then I just have coffee, or maybe toast, and then suddenly it’s later than I thought.\n\nSometimes I don’t notice until I’m trying to get dressed and my pants feel a little looser, and then I’m like, oh, I guess I haven’t really been eating actual meals. I keep buying things that are easy, but then I don’t make them, or I forget they’re there, and then I just eat whatever is closest. Coffee, toast, crackers, stuff like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling low on energy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to measure that. I mean, I get tired, but I also don’t know if that’s low energy or just… the day being too much.\n\nLike at work, sometimes I’ll sit down and open the thing I’m supposed to do, and then I end up rearranging the little sticky notes near my keyboard because one of them is curling up, and then I realize I haven’t actually started. But that’s not exactly energy, I guess.\n\nI haven’t really noticed it in a clear way, like “oh, I have no energy today.” It’s more… I lose momentum, maybe. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sleep has been pretty bad. Most nights I’ll get in bed and then I’m just lying there for hours, listening to the house make those little settling noises — like the pipes or the walls, I don’t know. I’ll think, okay, if I fall asleep now I can still get enough, and then I look again and it’s way later.\n\nAnd then even if I do sleep, I wake up around three or four a lot. That’s the worst part, because it’s not like I can just roll over and go back to sleep before work. I just lie there waiting for morning, basically.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. And with the house stuff, it’s worse, because I worked so hard to get it, and then if something needs fixing or I miss something or I don’t handle it right away, I start thinking I’m just… bad at being an adult, or a homeowner, like I don’t deserve to keep what I worked for.\n\nAnd then I’ll get stuck on things that probably aren’t even related, like how the mailbox looks crooked or whether the neighbors think I’m letting everything fall apart, and it turns into this whole thing. But yeah, mostly I just feel like everything is my fault somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… “hopeless” feels like a big word, I guess. Sometimes I feel like things are never going to get easier, especially when I’m just trying to keep up with normal stuff and it all feels heavier than it should.\n\nBut then I also get distracted by little things, like I’ll start thinking if I just organize one drawer or return one email then maybe I’ll feel less stuck, and then I’m thinking about office supplies for some reason. So… I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly. Maybe kind of discouraged? Like I can’t always see how it’s supposed to get better.\n\nDoctor: When these worries start, is it hard for you to stop or control them?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Sometimes I can stop them if something else interrupts me, like a meeting starting or my phone ringing. But if I’m just sitting there, it kind of keeps looping.\n\nAlthough… I don’t always know if I’m trying to control it or if I’m just following the thought, you know? Like I’ll think about one thing, then I’m thinking about something completely different, like whether I need more trash bags or if I forgot to move laundry, and then I’m not even sure what the original worry was.\n\nSo… maybe it is hard sometimes, but I don’t know how much. It’s not like I can measure it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worried like this on most days?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know if it’s most days. I’m not good at keeping track like that. Some weeks it feels like it’s there all the time, and then I’ll have a day where I’m busy and I don’t notice it as much.\n\nI guess mornings are weird because I’ll start thinking about what I have to do, but then I’m also thinking about whether I left dishes in the sink or if my shoes by the door look messy, and then I’m late because I’m looking for my badge. So it’s hard for me to say if that counts as “worried” or just… my brain being loud.\n\nMaybe it happens a lot, but I don’t want to say “most days” if I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I don’t know if I’d say all the time, but there are these moments where I realize I’ve been walking the same little route in my apartment, like from the kitchen to the front door and back again.\n\nAnd I’ll be rubbing my thumb against my fingers, like this repetitive thing, and checking the peephole even though nothing happened. It’s not like there’s a reason, exactly. I’ll just… keep doing it. Then I notice I’ve been doing it for a while and I feel kind of embarrassed, because I meant to be doing something else, like putting a cup away or getting my bag ready.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite being lower or higher than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been kind of all over the place. Mostly lower during the day, I think. Like I’ll mean to eat lunch and then I just have coffee, or maybe toast, and then suddenly it’s later than I thought.\n\nSometimes I don’t notice until I’m trying to get dressed and my pants feel a little looser, and then I’m like, oh, I guess I haven’t really been eating actual meals. I keep buying things that are easy, but then I don’t make them, or I forget they’re there, and then I just eat whatever is closest. Coffee, toast, crackers, stuff like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling low on energy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to measure that. I mean, I get tired, but I also don’t know if that’s low energy or just… the day being too much.\n\nLike at work, sometimes I’ll sit down and open the thing I’m supposed to do, and then I end up rearranging the little sticky notes near my keyboard because one of them is curling up, and then I realize I haven’t actually started. But that’s not exactly energy, I guess.\n\nI haven’t really noticed it in a clear way, like “oh, I have no energy today.” It’s more… I lose momentum, maybe. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sleep has been pretty bad. Most nights I’ll get in bed and then I’m just lying there for hours, listening to the house make those little settling noises — like the pipes or the walls, I don’t know. I’ll think, okay, if I fall asleep now I can still get enough, and then I look again and it’s way later.\n\nAnd then even if I do sleep, I wake up around three or four a lot. That’s the worst part, because it’s not like I can just roll over and go back to sleep before work. I just lie there waiting for morning, basically.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, sometimes when I’m really tired I think, like, I don’t want to keep doing the day over and over, if that makes sense, but then I start thinking about work emails or whether I paid my electric bill, and it gets kind of mixed up.\n\nI haven’t really sat down and made it into a… specific thought, I guess. “Hurting myself” sounds very direct, and I don’t know. I don’t think I can say it that way. It’s more like I get overwhelmed and want everything to stop for a while, but then I’ll be looking at my phone or folding a towel or something.\n\nDoctor: During this time, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when your depression felt clearly worse than usual most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s hard because it doesn’t feel like it started and stopped neatly, like a calendar block. It’s more like it settled in and stayed.\n\nThe depression and not really being interested in things has been there for… about two years and one month, I guess. Which sounds weirdly specific, but I remember because it lines up with when I kept thinking, “maybe this is just a rough month,” and then it wasn’t just a month.\n\nSo, yes, definitely more than two weeks. It’s been kind of most days for a long time.\n\nDoctor: During this time, was there ever a period when you felt clearly more depressed than your usual baseline?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s kind of hard for me to separate “worse than baseline” because the baseline itself has been low for so long. Like, it’s not like I was fine and then there was one obvious dip.\n\nThe depression and not wanting to do things, or not feeling much interest, has been around for about two years and one month. I remember thinking at first it was just a bad stretch, like when work gets busy and you stop doing normal stuff, but then it just… kept being there. So yes, there have been times it felt heavier, but honestly the whole thing has felt pretty consistently depressed for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually see yourself as not good enough?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the time, honestly. I came in partly because I can’t stop feeling like everything that goes wrong is somehow my fault.\n\nLike at work, if something is late or a number is off, I immediately think I’m a bad employee, even if it’s not totally on me. And then at home, with the house, it turns into this whole thing where I feel like I’m a bad homeowner too, like I don’t deserve to keep what I worked for. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but it feels really true in my head.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Sometimes I feel like things are never going to get easier, especially when I’m just trying to keep up with normal stuff and it all feels heavier than it should.\n\nBut then I also get distracted by little things, like I’ll start thinking if I just organize one drawer or return one email then maybe I’ll feel less stuck, and then I’m thinking about office supplies for some reason. So… I don’t know if I’d call it hopeless exactly. Maybe kind of discouraged? Sometimes I can stop them if something else interrupts me, like a meeting starting or my phone ringing. But if I’m just sitting there, it kind of keeps looping.\n\nAlthough… I don’t always know if I’m trying to control it or if I’m just following the thought, you know? Like I’ll think about one thing, then I’m thinking about something completely different, like whether I need more trash bags or if I forgot to move laundry, and then I’m not even sure what the original worry was.\n\nSo… maybe it is hard sometimes, but I don’t know how much. It’s not like I can measure it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worried like this on most days?\n\nPatient: I… don’t really know if it’s most days. I’m not good at keeping track like that. Some weeks it feels like it’s there all the time, and then I’ll have a day where I’m busy and I don’t notice it as much.\n\nI guess mornings are weird because I’ll start thinking about what I have to do, but then I’m also thinking about whether I left dishes in the sink or if my shoes by the door look messy, and then I’m late because I’m looking for my badge. So it’s hard for me to say if that counts as “worried” or just… my brain being loud.\n\nMaybe it happens a lot, but I don’t want to say “most days” if I’m not totally sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sometimes. I don’t know if I’d say all the time, but there are these moments where I realize I’ve been walking the same little route in my apartment, like from the kitchen to the front door and back again.\n\nAnd I’ll be rubbing my thumb against my fingers, like this repetitive thing, and checking the peephole even though nothing happened. It’s not like there’s a reason, exactly. I’ll just… keep doing it. Then I notice I’ve been doing it for a while and I feel kind of embarrassed, because I meant to be doing something else, like putting a cup away or getting my bag ready.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite being lower or higher than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s been kind of all over the place. Mostly lower during the day, I think. Like I’ll mean to eat lunch and then I just have coffee, or maybe toast, and then suddenly it’s later than I thought.\n\nSometimes I don’t notice until I’m trying to get dressed and my pants feel a little looser, and then I’m like, oh, I guess I haven’t really been eating actual meals. I keep buying things that are easy, but then I don’t make them, or I forget they’re there, and then I just eat whatever is closest. Coffee, toast, crackers, stuff like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling low on energy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to measure that. I mean, I get tired, but I also don’t know if that’s low energy or just… the day being too much.\n\nLike at work, sometimes I’ll sit down and open the thing I’m supposed to do, and then I end up rearranging the little sticky notes near my keyboard because one of them is curling up, and then I realize I haven’t actually started. But that’s not exactly energy, I guess.\n\nI haven’t really noticed it in a clear way, like “oh, I have no energy today.” It’s more… I lose momentum, maybe. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Yeah… sleep has been pretty bad. Most nights I’ll get in bed and then I’m just lying there for hours, listening to the house make those little settling noises — like the pipes or the walls, I don’t know. I’ll think, okay, if I fall asleep now I can still get enough, and then I look again and it’s way later.\n\nAnd then even if I do sleep, I wake up around three or four a lot. That’s the worst part, because it’s not like I can just roll over and go back to sleep before work. I just lie there waiting for morning, basically.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living, or thoughts of hurting yourself?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly. I mean, sometimes when I’m really tired I think, like, I don’t want to keep doing the day over and over, if that makes sense, but then I start thinking about work emails or whether I paid my electric bill, and it gets kind of mixed up.\n\nI haven’t really sat down and made it into a… specific thought, I guess. “Hurting myself” sounds very direct, and I don’t know. I don’t think I can say it that way. It’s more like I get overwhelmed and want everything to stop for a while, but then I’ll be looking at my phone or folding a towel or something.\n\nDoctor: During this time, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when your depression felt clearly worse than usual most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, it’s hard because it doesn’t feel like it started and stopped neatly, like a calendar block. It’s more like it settled in and stayed.\n\nThe depression and not really being interested in things has been there for… about two years and one month, I guess. Which sounds weirdly specific, but I remember because it lines up with when I kept thinking, “maybe this is just a rough month,” and then it wasn’t just a month.\n\nSo, yes, definitely more than two weeks. It’s been kind of most days for a long time.\n\nDoctor: During this time, was there ever a period when you felt clearly more depressed than your usual baseline?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s kind of hard for me to separate “worse than baseline” because the baseline itself has been low for so long. Like, it’s not like I was fine and then there was one obvious dip.\n\nThe depression and not wanting to do things, or not feeling much interest, has been around for about two years and one month. I remember thinking at first it was just a bad stretch, like when work gets busy and you stop doing normal stuff, but then it just… kept being there. So yes, there have been times it felt heavier, but honestly the whole thing has felt pretty consistently depressed for a long time.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually see yourself as not good enough?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most of the time, honestly. I came in partly because I can’t stop feeling like everything that goes wrong is somehow my fault.\n\nLike at work, if something is late or a number is off, I immediately think I’m a bad employee, even if it’s not totally on me. And then at home, with the house, it turns into this whole thing where I feel like I’m a bad homeowner too, like I don’t deserve to keep what I worked for. Which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but it feels really true in my head.\n\nDoctor: During the past two years, have you felt this way about yourself on most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I think. It’s not always loud, but it’s kind of there in the background.\n\nLike if something goes wrong at work, even something small, I immediately go to, “This is because I’m not good enough,” or “I’m a bad employee.” And then it spills over into stuff at home too. I’ll look around the house and think I’m messing that up too, like I don’t deserve what I worked for, which… I know that probably sounds extreme.\n\nBut yeah, over the past couple years, that feeling has been there a lot. Most days.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
